Run and Frequency quotas for q q -binary trials
Jungtaek Oh

Abstract
We study the distributions of waiting times in variations of the q q -sooner and later waiting time problem. One variation imposes length and frequency quotas on the runs of successes and failures. Another case considers binary trials for which the probability of ones is geometrically varying. First, we consider sooner case (or later case) impose the run quotas both runs of successes and failures. Theorem 3.1 gives an probability function of the q q -sooner waiting time distribution of order ( k 1 , k 2 ) (k_{1},k_{2}) when a quota is imposed on runs of successes and failures. Theorem 3.2 gives an probability function of the q q -later waiting time distribution of order ( k 1 , k 2 ) (k_{1},k_{2}) when a quota is imposed on runs of successes and failures. Next, we consider sooner case (or later case) impose a frequency quota on successes and a run quota on failures. Theorem 4.1 gives an probability function of the q q -sooner waiting time distribution impose a frequency quota on successes and a run quota on failures. Theorem 4.2 gives an probability function of the q q -later waiting time distribution impose a frequency quota on successes and a run quota on failures. Next, we consider sooner case (or later case) impose a run quota on successes and a frequency quota on failures. Theorem 4.3 gives an probability function of the q q -sooner waiting time distribution impose a run quota on successes and a frequency quota on failures. Theorem 4.4 gives an probability function of the q q -later waiting time distribution impose a run quota on successes and a frequency quota on failures. Next, we consider sooner case (or later case) impose a frequency quotas on successes and failures. Theorem 5.1 gives an probability function of the q q -sooner waiting time distribution impose a frequency quotas on successes and failures. Theorem 5.3 gives an probability function of the q q -later waiting time distribution impose a frequency quotas on both successes and failures. We also study the distributions of Longest run under the same variations. The main theorems are sooner and later waiting time problem and the joint distribution of the length of the longest success and longest failure runs when a run and frequency quotas imposed on runs of successes and failure. In the present work, we consider a sequence of independent binary zero and one trials with not necessarily identical distributed with probability of ones varying according to a geometric rule. Exact formulae for the distributions obtained by means of enumerative combinatorics.



Keywords:
Sooner/later waiting time problems, Run quota, Frequency quota, Longest run, Runs, Binary trials,
q
-Distributions

1
Introduction

Charalambides, 2010b
studied discrete
q
-distributions on Bernoulli trials with a geometrically varying success probability. Let us consider a sequence
X_{1}
,…,
X_{n}
of zero(failure)-one(success) Bernoulli trials, such that the trials of the subsequence after the
(i-1)
st zero until the
i
th zero are independent with equal failure probability. For
i>0
the
i
’s geometric sequences of trials is the subsequence after the
(i-1)
’st zero. The probability
q_{i}
of zero is fixed for all trials during the
i
’th geometric sequence and is given by

\begin{split}q_{i}=1-\theta q^{i-1},\quad 0\leq\theta\leq 1,\quad 0\leq q<1.\end{split}

(1.1)

By the standard definitions it follows from (
1.1
) that the success probability
1-q_{i}
is geometrically decreasing. Let
S_{j}^{(0)}=\Sigma_{m=1}^{j}(1-X_{m})
denote the number of zeros in the first
j
trials. Because the probability of zeros at the
i
th geometric sequence of trials is in fact the conditional probability
q_{j,i}
of occurrence of a zero at any trial
j
given the occurrence of exactly
i-1
zeros in the previous trials. We can write as follows.

\begin{split}q_{j,i}=p\Big(X_{j}=0\ \bigm|\ S_{j-1}^{(0)}=i-1\Big)=1-\theta q^{i-1},\quad\text{for}\ i\leq j.\end{split}

(1.2)

To make the preceding more clear, we consider the example of the sequence 111011110111100110 for
n=18
. Each subsequence in brackets has own success and failure probabilities according to the geometric rule.

This stochastic model (
1.1
) or (
1.2
) has interesting applications, studied as a reliability growth model by
Dubman and Sherman, 1969
, and applies to a
q
-boson theory in physics by
Jing and Fan, 1994
and
Jing, 1994
. This stochastic model (
1.1
) also applies to start-up demonstration tests, as a sequential-intervention model which is proposed by
Balakrishnan et al., 1995
.

The stochastic model (
1.1
) is the
q
-analogue of the classical binomial distribution with geometrically varying probabilities of zeros. This provides a stochastic model for independent identically distributed (IID) trials with failure probability function
\pi
given by

\pi_{j}=P\left(X_{j}=0\right)=1-\theta,\ j>0,

(1.3)

where
0\leq\theta<1
.

As
q
tends toward
1
, the stochastic model (
1.1
) reduces to IID(Bernoulli) model (
1.3
), since
q_{i}\rightarrow\pi_{i}
,
i=1,2,\ldots
or
q_{j,i}\rightarrow 1-\theta
,
i=1,2,\ldots,j
,
j=1,2,\ldots.

The discrete
q
-distributions based on the stochastic model of the sequence of independent Bernoulli trials have been investigated by numerous researchers, for a lucid review and comprehensive list of publications on this area the interested reader may consult the monographs by
Charalambides, 2010b
;
Charalambides, 2010a
;
Charalambides, 2016
.

From a Mathematical and Statistical point of view,
Charalambides, 2016
mentioned in the preface of his book
”It should be noticed that a stochastic model of a sequence of independent Bernoulli trials, in which the probability of success at a trial is assumed to vary with the number of trials and/or the number of successes, is advantageous in the sense that it permits incorporating the experience gained from previous trials and/or successes. If the probability of success at a trial is a very general function of the number of trials and/or the number successes, very little can be inferred from it about the distributions of the various random variables that may be defined on this model. The assumption that the probability of success (or failure) at a trial varies geometrically, with rate (proportion)
q
, leads to the introduction of discrete
q
-distributions”
.

Following the work of
Philippou and Muwafi, 1982
,
Philippou et al., 1983
and
Aki et al., 1984
, the distribution theory of runs and patterns has been intensively developed in the last few decades, because of its theoretical interest and applications in a wide variety of research areas such as hypothesis testing, system reliability, quality control, physics, psychology, radar astronomy, molecular biology, computer science, insurance, and finance. During the past few decades up to recently, the meaningful progress on runs and pattern statistics has been wonderfully surveyed in
Balakrishnan and Koutras, 2003
as well as in
Fu and Lou, 2003
and references therein. Furthermore, there are some more recent contributions on the topic such as
Arapis et al., 2018
,
Eryilmaz, 2018
,
Kong, 2019
,
Makri et al., 2019
, and
Aki, 2019
.

Waiting time distributions related to runs and patterns received much attention recently in applied probability. In many situations, it is necessary to study the waiting time problems associated with two or more runs or patterns; for example, an experiment stops or a system fails whenever one of several predefined runs or patterns occurs. Sometimes, the order among these runs or patterns is important and must be taken into consideration; for example, the DNA sequence of a virus contains certain patterns that occur in order. We introduce another important class of waiting time distributions called sooner waiting time distributions. We will consider both success and failure runs of fixed lengths, instead of simply focus on success runs of fixed length. An interesting type of distribution is the waiting time until the first occurrence of a success run of fixed length or a failure run of fixed length in a sequence of Bernoulli trials was introduced by
Feller, 1968
for the first time, instead of just concentrate on success runs of fixed length. The related problems, named as
sooner/later succession
or
run quota problems
. Let
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots
be a sequence of binary trials, each resulting in either a Success (S) or Failure (F) and fix two positive integers
k_{1}\geq 1
,
k_{2}\geq 1
. Let
T_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{(S)}
denote the waiting time until a sequence of
k_{1}
consecutive successes or
k_{2}
consecutive failures are observed for the first time.

The distribution of
T_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{(S)}
named as
sooner succession quota waiting time distribution
, or
sooner run quota waiting time distribution
, or simply
sooner waiting time distribution
by
Ebneshahrashoob and Sobel, 1990
.
Ling, 1990
named as
geometric distribution of order

(k_{1},k_{2})
.

The sooner waiting time random variable
T_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{(S)}
is closely related with the random variable
T_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{(L)}
, which denote stopping the trials when both a success run of length
k_{1}
and a failure run of length
k_{2}
have been observed. This distribution named as
later succession quota waiting time distribution
, or
later run quota waiting time distribution
, or simply
later waiting time distribution
.

Feller, 1968
applied renewal theory to derive the p.g.f. of sooner waiting time distribution by using recurrent events.
Ebneshahrashoob and Sobel, 1990
first introduced the generalized probability generating function (gpgf), which provides a mechanism to take into account certain partitions into the probability space of the random variable of interest. They investigated probability generating functions of the sooner and later waiting time problems.
Ling, 1992
and
Sobel and Ebneshahrashoob, 1992
studied the sooner and later problems when a frequency quota is imposed on runs of successes and failures.
Aki et al., 1993
derived the p.g.f. of the sooner and later waiting time problems in the first order Markov dependent trials.
Balasubramanian et al., 1993
studied sooner and later waiting time problems in a markov correlated Bernoulli trials.
Aki et al., 1996
studied sooner and later waiting time problems in a higher order Markov dependent trials.
Aki and Hirano, 1999
studied sooner and later waiting time problems in a Markov dependent bivariate trials.
Koutras and Alexandrou, 1997
studied the problem in the trinary trials.

Han and Aki, 1999
studied sooner and later waiting problems for runs in both identical and nonidentical independent sequences of multi-state trials.
Han and Hirano, 2003
investigated sooner and later waiting time problems for patterns in multi-state Markov dependent trials.
Kim et al., 2013
derived probabilty distribution of sooner waiting time for both independent and homogeneous two-state Markovian Bernoulli trials, using a generalized Fibonacci sequence of order
k
.
Serkan et al., 2016
studied the generalized sooner waiting time problems in a sequence of trinary trials with random rewards.
Kim and Kim, 2019
investigated the sooner waiting time problems in a sequence of independent multi-state trials with random rewards, using the analysis of a Markov chain with a special transition structure. They are generalized the sooner waiting time problems of
Serkan et al., 2016
for a sequence of trinary trials with random rewards in the case of multi-state trials.

The random variable is the
L_{n}
length of the longest success run in
n
trials. Longest runs which can be considered as the extremes of run lengths. Denote by
R_{n}^{(j)}
is the number of runs of type
j

(j=0,1)
and let
x_{i}(y_{i})
denote the length of the
i
th run of successes (failures) in
X_{1}
,
\ldots
,
X_{n}
, respectively. Let
L_{n}^{(j)}
is denote denote the longest run of type
j
(
j=0,1
). More precisely, a fixed number
n
,

\begin{split}L_{n}^{(j)}&=\text{max}\Big\{b\ \bigm|\ X_{a+1}=\cdots=X_{a+b}=j\ \wedge\ 0\leq a\leq n-b\Big\}\\
&=\underset{1\leq j\leq R_{n}^{(j)}}{\text{max}}x_{j}.\end{split}

To make more clear we mention by way of example that a sequence of ten trials
0011110110
. We have
R_{10}^{(0)}=3
,
R_{10}^{(1)}=2
,
L_{n}^{(0)}=2
and
L_{n}^{(1)}=4
.

Burr and Cane, 1961
studied the exact expression for the distribution of the longest run in i.i.d. Bernoulli trials.
Fu and Koutras, 1994
obtain the distribution of the longest run, using a Markov chain imbedding technique.
Fu, 1996
developed a method of finite Markov-chain imbedding technique to obtain the exact distribution of
L_{n}
in sequences of multi-state trials.
Lou, 1996
studied the joint distribution of the length of the longest success run and the number of successes, as well as the conditional distribution of the length of the longest success run given the number of successes in sequences of Bernoulli trials.
Vaggelatou, 2003
investigated the asymptotic results for the longest run statistics in a multi state markov chain.
Makri et al., 2007
derived the exact probability function of the the longest run statistics for both linear and circular sequence by means of combinatorial analysis. Many authors have contributed to the longest run statistics in the cases of Bernoulli trials, Markov dependent trials, and exchangeable binary trials. See.
Philippou and Makri, 1985
;
Philippou and Makri, 1986
;
Philippou and Makri, 1990
,
Antzoulakos and Philippou, 1997
;
Antzoulakos and Philippou, 1998
,
Charalambides, 1994
,
Serkan, 2005
;
Eryilmaz, 2005
;
Eryilmaz, 2006
,
Muselli, 1996
.

As mentioned in
Johnson et al., 2005
, ”
The formulas for the probabilities for these distributions are not at first sight very illuminating; also in some cases there is more than one correct published expression for the probabilities
”.
Fu and Koutras, 1994
mentioned that some of the formulae are extremely complicated and hard to compute. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work that derive the exact probability functions of sooner and later waiting time problem and longest run statistics in the binary trials with probability of ones varying according to a geometric rule.

In this paper we study of the
q
-sooner/later waiting time distribution of order
(k_{1},k_{2})
and longest run statistics when a quota is imposed on runs of successes and failures, with probability of ones varying according to a geometric rule. Run (or succession) quota is based on the length of the current run in that trial and frequency quota is based on the total frequency accumulated in that trial (i.e. number of the totality of frequency).

The paper is organized as follows. In section 2 we introduce basic definitions and necessary notations that will be useful throughout this article. In Section 3 we shall study of the
q
-sooner and later waiting time distribution of order
(k_{1},k_{2})
when a quota is imposed on runs of successes and failures. We derive exact probability function of
q
-sooner and later waiting time by means of combinatorial analysis. In section 4 we shall study of the
q
-sooner and later waiting times to be discussed will arise by setting quotas on both runs and frequencies of successes and failures. First, we consider sooner cases (or later cases) impose a frequency quota on successes and a run quota on failures. Next, we consider sooner cases (or later cases) impose a run quota on successes and a frequency quota on failures. In section 5 we study the joint distribution of the lengths of the longest success and longest failure runs.

2
Terminology and notation

We first recall some definitions, notation and known results in which will be used in this paper. Throughout the paper, we suppose that
0<q<1
. First, we introduce the following notation.

•

L_{n}^{(1)}:
the length of the longest run of successes in
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}
;

•

L_{n}^{(0)}:
the length of the longest run of failures in
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}
;

•

S_{n}:
the total number of successes in
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}
;

•

F_{n}:
the total number of failures in
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}
.

Next, let us introduce some basic
q
-sequences and functions and their properties, which are useful in the sequel. The
q
-shifted factorials are defined as

(a;q)_{0}=1,\quad(a;q)_{n}=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(1-aq^{k}),\quad(a;q)_{\infty}=\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}(1-aq^{k}).

(2.1)

Let
m
,
n
and
i
be positive integer and
z
and
q
be real numbers, with
q\neq 1
. The number
[z]_{q}=(1-q^{z})/(1-q)
is called
q
-number and in particular
[z]_{q}
is called
q
-integer. The
m
th order factorial of the
q
-number
[z]_{q}
, which is defined by

\begin{split}[z]_{m,q}&=\prod_{i=1}^{m}[z-i+1]_{q}=[z]_{q}[z-1]_{q}\cdots[z-m+1]_{q}\\
&=\frac{(1-q^{z})(1-q^{z-1})\cdots(1-q^{z-m+1})}{(1-q)^{m}},\ z=1,2,\ldots,\ m=0,1,\ldots,z.\end{split}

(2.2)

is called
q
-factorial of
z
of order
m
. In particular,
[m]_{q}!=[1]_{q}[2]_{q}...[m]_{q}
is called
q
-factorial of
m
. The
q
-binomial coefficient (or Gaussian polynomial) is defined by

\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}n\\
m\end{bmatrix}_{q}&=\frac{[n]_{m,q}}{[m]_{q}!}=\frac{[n]_{q}!}{[m]_{q}![n-m]_{q}!}=\frac{(1-q^{n})(1-q^{n-1})\cdots(1-q^{n-m+1})}{(1-q^{m})(1-q^{m-1})\cdots(1-q)}\\
&=\frac{(q;q)_{n}}{(q;q)_{m}(q;q)_{n-m}},\ m=1,2,\ldots,\end{split}

(2.3)

The
q
-binomial (
q
-Newton’s binomial) formula is expressed as

\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1+zq^{i-1})=\sum_{k=0}^{n}q^{k(k-1)/2}\begin{bmatrix}n\\
k\end{bmatrix}_{q}z^{k},\ -\infty<z<\infty,\ n=1,2,\ldots.

(2.4)

For
q\rightarrow 1
the
q
-analogs tend to their classical counterparts, that is

\begin{split}\lim\limits_{q\rightarrow 1}\begin{bmatrix}n\\
r\end{bmatrix}_{q}={n\choose r}\end{split}

Let us consider again a sequence of independent geometric sequences of trials with probability of failure at the
i
th geometric sequence of trials given by (
1.1
) or (
1.2
). We are interesting now is focused on the study of the number of successes in a given number of trials in this stochastic model.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
Z_{n}
be the number of successes in a sequence of
n
independent Bernoulli trials, with probability of success at the
i
th geometric sequence of trials given by (
1.1
) or (
1.2
). The distribution of the random variable
Z_{n}
is called
q
-binomial distribution, with parameters
n
,
\theta
, and
q
.

Let we introduce a
q
-analogue of the binomial distribution with the probability function of the number
Z_{n}
of successes in
n
trials
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
is given by

\begin{split}P_{q,\theta}\{Z_{n}=r\}=\begin{bmatrix}n\\
r\end{bmatrix}_{q}\theta^{r}\prod_{i=1}^{n-r}(1-\theta q^{i-1}),\end{split}

(2.5)

for
r=0,1,...,n
,
0<q<1
. The distribution is called a
q
-binomial distribution. For
q\rightarrow 1
, because

\begin{split}\lim\limits_{q\rightarrow 1}\begin{bmatrix}n\\
r\end{bmatrix}_{q}={n\choose r}\end{split}

so that the q-binomial distribution converges to the usual binomial distribution as
q\rightarrow 1
, as follows

P_{\theta}\left(Z_{n}=r\right)={n\choose r}\theta^{r}(1-\theta)^{n-r},\ r=0,1,\ldots,n,

(2.6)

with parameters
n
and
\theta
.
The
q
-binomial distribution studied by
Charalambides, 2010b
;
Charalambides, 2016
, which is connected with
q
-Berstein polynomial.
Jing, 1994
introduced probability function (
2.5
) as a
q
-deformed binomial distribution, also derived recurrence relation of its probability distribution.
In the sequel,
P_{q,\theta}(.)
and
P_{\theta}(.)
denote probabilities related with the stochastic model (
1.1
) and (
1.3
), respectively.

3
Succession (run) quota for
q
-binary trials

In this section, we shall study of the
q
-sooner and later waiting time distribution of order
(k_{1},k_{2})
when a quota is imposed on runs of successes and failures. Let
E_{1}
be the event that a
k_{1}
consecutive successes (ones) occurs, and
E_{0}
be the event that a
k_{2}
consecutive failures (zeros) occurs.

Let
W
be a nonnegative integer valued random variable defined on a probability space
(\Omega,F,P).
Suppose that we are given a partition of
\Omega
, i.e.
\Omega
can be written as a disjoint union of finite or countably many subsets
\Omega_{i}
of
\Omega
. Then the PMF of event
W=n
can be expressed as

\begin{split}P_{q,\theta}(W=n)=\sum P_{q,\theta}\left(\{\omega\in\Omega:W(\omega)=n\}\right),\ 0<\theta<1,\ 0<q<1.\end{split}

First, we solve the sooner waiting time problem by means of combinatorial analysis. Let
W_{S}
be a random variable denoting that the waiting time until either
k_{1}
consecutive successes or
k_{2}
consecutive failures are occurred, whichever event observe sooner. To be precise, we let
\Omega_{1}=\{\omega\in\Omega|\omega_{n}\in E_{1}\setminus E_{2}\}
and
\Omega_{2}=\{\omega_{n}\in\Omega|\omega\in E_{2}\setminus E_{1}\},
where
\omega_{n}
for every
\omega
as the string of the first
n
bits of
\omega
. The support (range set) of
W_{S}
is

\begin{split}\mathfrak{R}(W_{S})=\{\text{min}(k_{1},k_{2}),\ldots\}.\end{split}

Next, we solve the later waiting time problem by means of combinatorial analysis. Let
W_{L}
be a random variable denoting that the waiting time until both of
k_{1}
consecutive successes and
k_{2}
consecutive failures are occurred, whichever event observe later. To be precise, we let
\Omega_{3}=\{\omega_{n}\in\Omega|\Omega_{2}\cap E_{1}\}
and
\Omega_{4}=\{\omega_{n}\in\Omega|\Omega_{1}\cap E_{2}\}.
The support (range set) of
W_{L}
is

\begin{split}\mathfrak{R}(W_{L})=\{k_{1}+k_{2},\ldots\}.\end{split}

As an illustration we mention that, suppose that
E_{1}
means four consecutive ones:
1111
, and
E_{2}
means three consecutive zeros:
000
. And we consider a bitstring
S=11010001001111000\ldots
. Then
S
belongs to both events
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
. But the beginning string
S^{\prime}=1101000
belongs only to
E_{2}
and not to
E_{1}
, so it belongs to
E_{2}\setminus E_{1}
, and the definition of
\Omega_{2}
can be expressed by saying this precisely. Similarly the beginning string
S"=11010001001111
belongs to the intersection of
\Omega_{2}
and
E_{1}
, which means that
E_{1}
comes later than
E_{2}
in
S
.

3.1
q
-sooner waiting time distribution of order
(k_{1},k_{2})

The problem of waiting time that will be discussed in this section is one of the ’sooner cases’ and it emerges when a run quota is imposed on runs of successes and failures. More specifically, Binary (zero and one) trials with probability of ones varying according to a geometric rule, are performed sequentially until
k_{1}
consecutive successes or
k_{2}
consecutive failures are observed, whichever event occurs first. Let
W_{S}^{(1)}
be a random variable denoting that the waiting time until
k_{1}
consecutive successes are observed for the first time and no failure run of length
k_{2}
or more has appeared before and
W_{S}^{(0)}
be a random variable denoting that the waiting time until
k_{2}
consecutive failures are observed for the first time and no success run of length
k_{1}
or more has appeared before.

We will also simply write
f^{(1)}_{q,S}(n;\theta)=P_{q,\theta}(W_{S}^{(1)}=n)
and
f^{(0)}_{q,S}(n;\theta)=P_{q,\theta}(W_{S}^{(0)}=n)
. Therefore we can write the probability of event
W_{S}=n
as follows

\begin{split}P_{q,\theta}(W_{S}=n)=f^{(1)}_{q,S}(n;\theta)+f^{(0)}_{q,S}(n;\theta),\ n\geq\text{min}(k_{1},\ k_{2}).\end{split}

We now make some useful Definition and Lemma for the proofs of Theorem in the sequel.

Definition 3.1
.

For
0<q\leq 1
, we define

\begin{split}A_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)={\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}}}\ \sum_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}}q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s-1}},\ \end{split}

where the summation is over all integers
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1},
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
satisfying

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s-1,\end{split}

\begin{split}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=m,\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s,\end{split}

\begin{split}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=r.\end{split}

The following gives a recurrence relation useful for the computation of
A_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
.

Lemma 3.1
.

For
0<q\leq 1
,
A_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
obeys the following recurrence relation.

\begin{split}A&{}_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
&=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}q^{a(r-b)}A_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m-a,r-b,s-1)&\text{for}\ s>1,\ s-1\leq m\leq(s-1)(k_{1}-1)\\
&\text{and}\ s\leq r\leq s(k_{2}-1),\\
1&\text{for}\ s=1,\ m=0,\ \text{and}\ 1\leq r\leq k_{2}-1,\text{or}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{split}

Proof.

For
s>1
,
s-1\leq m\leq(s-1)(k_{1}-1)
and
s\leq r\leq s(k_{2}-1)
, we observe that since
x_{s-1}
can assume the values
1,\ldots,k_{1}-1
, then
A_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
can be written as

\begin{split}A&{}_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
=&\sum_{x_{s-1}=1}^{k_{1}-1}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-2}=m-x_{s-1}\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-2}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}}\quad{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=r\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}}q^{x_{s-1}(r-y_{s})}\ q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-2}}.\end{split}

Similarly, we observe that since
y_{s}
can assume the values
1,\ldots,k_{2}-1
, then
A_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
can be rewritten as

\begin{split}A&{}_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
=&\sum_{y_{s}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{x_{s-1}=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{x_{s-1}(r-y_{s})}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-2}=m-x_{s-1}\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-2}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}}\quad{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}=r-y_{s}\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}}q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-2}}\\
=&\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}q^{a(r-b)}A_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m-a,r-b,s-1).\end{split}

The other cases are obvious and thus the proof is completed.
∎

Remark 1
.

We observe that
A_{1}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
is the number of integer solutions
(x_{1},\ \ldots,\ x_{s-1})
and
(y_{1},\ \ldots,\ y_{s})
of

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s-1,\end{split}

\begin{split}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=m,\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s,\end{split}

\begin{split}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=r\end{split}

which is

\begin{split}A_{1}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)=S(s-1,\ k_{1},\ m)S(s,\ k_{2},\ r),\end{split}

where
S(a,\ b,\ c)
denotes the total number of integer solutions
x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{a}=c
such that
0<x_{i}<b
for
i=1,2,\ldots,a
. Alternatively, it is the number of ways of distributing
c
identical balls into
a
different cells with no containing more or equal than
b
balls. The number can be expressed as

\begin{split}S(a,\ b,\ c)=\sum_{j=0}^{min(a,\left[\frac{c-a}{b-1}\right])}(-1)^{j}{a\choose j}{c-j(b-1)-1\choose a-1}.\end{split}

See, e.g.
Charalambides, 2002
.

Definition 3.2
.

For
0<q\leq 1
, we define

\begin{split}B_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)={\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}}}\ \sum_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}}q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s}},\ \end{split}

where the summation is over all integers
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
satisfying

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s,\end{split}

\begin{split}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=m,\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s,\end{split}

\begin{split}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=r.\end{split}

The following gives a recurrence relation useful for the computation of
B_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For
0<q\leq 1
,
B_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
obeys the following recurrence relation.

\begin{split}B&{}_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
&=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}q^{a(r-b)}B_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m-a,r-b,s-1)&\text{for}\ s>1,\ s\leq m\leq s(k_{1}-1)\\
&\text{and}\ s\leq r\leq s(k_{2}-1),\\
1&\text{for}\ s=1,\ 1\leq m\leq k_{1}-1\\
&\text{and}\ 1\leq r\leq k_{2}-1,\text{or}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{split}

Proof.

For
s>1
,
s\leq m\leq s(k_{1}-1)
and
s\leq r\leq s(k_{2}-1)
, we observe that since
x_{s}
can assume the values
1,\ldots,k_{1}-1
, then
B_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
can be written as

\begin{split}B&{}_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
=&\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{k_{1}-1}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=m-x_{s}\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}}\quad{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=r\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}}q^{x_{s}(r-y_{s})}\ q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}.\end{split}

Similarly, we observe that since
y_{s}
can assume the values
1,\ldots,k_{2}-1
, then
B_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
can be rewritten as

\begin{split}B&{}_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
=&\sum_{y_{s}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{x_{s}(r-y_{s})}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=m-x_{s}\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}}\quad{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}=r-y_{s}\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}}\ q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}\\
=&\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}q^{a(r-b)}B_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m-a,r-b,s-1).\end{split}

The other cases are obvious and thus the proof is completed.
∎

Remark 2
.

We observe that
B_{1}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
is the number of integer solutions
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
and
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})
of

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s,\end{split}

\begin{split}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=m,\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s,\end{split}

\begin{split}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=r\end{split}

which is

\begin{split}B_{1}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)=S(s,\ k_{1},\ m)S(s,\ k_{2},\ r),\end{split}

where
S(a,\ b,\ c)
denotes the total number of integer solutions
x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{a}=c
such that
0<x_{i}<b
for
i=1,2,\ldots,a
. The number can be expressed as

\begin{split}S(a,\ b,\ c)=\sum_{j=0}^{a}(-1)^{j}{a\choose j}{c-j(b-1)-1\choose a-1}.\end{split}

See, e.g.
Charalambides, 2002
.

Definition 3.3
.

For
0<q\leq 1
, we define

\begin{split}C_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)={\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}}}\ \sum_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}}q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s}},\ \end{split}

where the summation is over all integers
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}
satisfying

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s,\end{split}

\begin{split}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=m,\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s-1,\end{split}

\begin{split}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}=r.\end{split}

The following gives a recurrence relation useful for the computation of
C_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
.

Lemma 3.3
.

For
0<q\leq 1
,
C_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
obeys the following recurrence relation.

\begin{split}C&{}_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
&=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}q^{ra}C_{q}(m-a,r-b,s-1)&\text{for}\ s>1,\ s\leq m\leq s(k_{1}-1)\\
&\text{and}\ s-1\leq r\leq(s-1)(k_{2}-1),\\
1&\text{for}\ s=1,\ 1\leq m\leq k_{1}-1\ \text{and}\ r=0,\text{or}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{split}

Proof.

For
s>1
,
s\leq m\leq s(k_{1}-1)
and
s-1\leq r\leq(s-1)(k_{2}-1),
we observe that since
x_{s}
can assume the values
1,\ldots,k_{1}-1
, then
C_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
can be written as

\begin{split}C&{}_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
=&\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{k_{1}-1}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=m-x_{s}\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}}\quad{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=r\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}}q^{rx_{s}}\ q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}.\end{split}

Similarly, we observe that since
y_{s}
can assume the values
1,\ldots,k_{2}-1
, then
C_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
can be rewritten as

\begin{split}C&{}_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
=&\sum_{y_{s}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{rx_{s}}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=m-x_{s}\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}}\quad{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}=r-y_{s}\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}}\ q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}\\
=&\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}q^{ra}C_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m-a,r-b,s-1).\end{split}

The other cases are obvious and thus the proof is completed.
∎

Remark 3
.

We observe that
C_{1}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
is the number of integer solutions
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
and
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})
of

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s,\end{split}

\begin{split}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=m,\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s-1,\end{split}

\begin{split}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}=r\end{split}

which is

\begin{split}C_{1}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)=S(s,\ k_{1},\ m)S(s-1,\ k_{2},\ r),\end{split}

where
S(a,\ b,\ c)
denotes the total number of integer solutions
x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{a}=c
such that
0<x_{i}<b
for
i=1,\ 2,\ldots,a
. The number can be expressed as

\begin{split}S(a,\ b,\ c)=\sum_{j=0}^{a}(-1)^{j}{a\choose j}{c-j(b-1)-1\choose a-1}.\end{split}

See, e.g.
Charalambides, 2002
..

Definition 3.4
.

For
0<q\leq 1
, we define

\begin{split}D_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)={\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}}}\ \sum_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}}q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s-1}+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s})x_{s}},\ \end{split}

where the summation is over all integers
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
satisfying

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s,\end{split}

\begin{split}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=m,\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s,\end{split}

\begin{split}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=r.\end{split}

The following gives a recurrence relation useful for the computation of
D_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
.

Lemma 3.4
.

For
0<q\leq 1
,
D_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
obeys the following recurrence relation.

\begin{split}D&{}_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
&=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}q^{ra}D_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m-a,r-b,s-1)&\text{for}\ s>1,\ s\leq m\leq s(k_{1}-1)\\
&\text{and}\ s\leq r\leq s(k_{2}-1),\\
1&\text{for}\ s=1,\ 1\leq m\leq k_{1}-1\\
&\text{and}\ 1\leq r\leq k_{2}-1,\text{or}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{split}

Proof.

For
s>1
,
s\leq m\leq s(k_{1}-1)
and
s\leq r\leq s(k_{2}-1)
, we observe that since
x_{s}
can assume the values
1,\ldots,k_{1}-1
, then
D_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
can be written as

\begin{split}D&{}_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
=&\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{k_{1}-1}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=m-x_{s}\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}}\quad{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=r\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}}q^{rx_{s}}\ q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s-1}}.\end{split}

Similarly, we observe that since
y_{s}
can assume the values
1,\ldots,k_{2}-1
, then
D_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
can be rewritten as

\begin{split}D&{}_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
=&\sum_{y_{s}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{rx_{s}}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=m-x_{s}\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}}\quad{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}=r-y_{s}\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}}\ q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s-1}}\\
=&\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}q^{ra}D_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m-a,r-b,s-1).\end{split}

The other cases are obvious and thus the proof is completed.
∎

Remark 4
.

We observe that
D_{1}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)
is the number of integer solutions
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
and
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})
of

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s,\end{split}

\begin{split}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=m,\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s,\end{split}

\begin{split}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=r\end{split}

which is

\begin{split}D_{1}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(m,r,s)=S(s,\ k_{1},\ m)S(s,\ k_{2},\ r),\end{split}

where
S(a,\ b,\ c)
denotes the total number of integer solutions
x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{a}=c
such that
0<x_{i}<b
for
i=1,2,\ldots,a
. The number can be expressed as

\begin{split}S(a,\ b,\ c)=\sum_{j=0}^{a}(-1)^{j}{a\choose j}{c-j(b-1)-1\choose a-1}.\end{split}

See, e.g.
Charalambides, 2002
.

The probability function of the
q
-sooner waiting time distribution of order
(k_{1},k_{2})
when a quota is imposed on runs of successes and failures is obtained by the following theorem. It is evident that

P_{q,\theta}(W_{S}=n)=0\ \text{for}\ 0\leq n<\text{min}(k_{1},k_{2})

and so we shall focus on determining the probability mass function for
n\geq\min(k_{1},\ k_{2})
.

Theorem 3.1
.

The PMF
f_{q,S}(n;\theta)=P_{q,\theta}(W_{S}=n)
satisfies

\begin{split}f_{q,S}(n;\theta)=f^{(1)}_{q,S}(n;\theta)+f^{(0)}_{q,S}(n;\theta),\ \text{for}\ n\geq\min(k_{1},\ k_{2}),\end{split}

where
f^{(1)}_{q,S}(k_{1};\theta)=\theta^{k_{1}}
,
f^{(0)}_{q,S}(k_{2};\theta)={\prod_{j=1}^{k_{2}}}(1-\theta q^{j-1})
,

\begin{split}f^{(1)}_{q,S}(n;\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{1}}\theta^{n-i}q^{ik_{1}}{\prod_{j=1}^{i}}(1-\theta q^{j-1})\sum_{s=1}^{i}\bigg[&A_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{1}-i,\ i,\ s)\\
&+B_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{1}-i,\ i,\ s)\bigg],\ n>k_{1}\\
\end{split}

and

\begin{split}f^{(0)}_{q,S}(n;\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{2}}\theta^{n-k_{2}-i}{\prod_{j=1}^{i+k_{2}}}(1-\theta q^{j-1})\sum_{s=1}^{n-k_{2}-i}\bigg[&C_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{2}-i,\ i,\ s)\\
&+D_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{2}-i,\ i,\ s)\bigg],\ n>k_{2}.\\
\end{split}

Proof.

We start with the study of
P_{q,S}^{(1)}(n)
. It is easy to see that
P_{q,S}^{(1)}(k_{1})=\big(\theta q^{0}\big)^{k_{1}}=\theta^{k_{1}}
. From now on we assume
n>k_{1},
and we can rewrite
P_{q,S}^{(1)}(n)
as follows

\begin{split}P_{q,S}^{(1)}(n)&=P_{q,\theta}\left(L_{n-k_{1}}^{(1)}<k_{1}\ \wedge\ L_{n-k_{1}}^{(0)}<k_{2}\ \wedge\ X_{n-k_{1}}=0\ \wedge\ X_{n-k_{1}+1}=\cdots=X_{n}=1\right).\\
\end{split}

We partition the event
W_{S}^{(1)}=n
into disjoint events given by
F_{n-k_{1}}=i,
for
i=1,\ldots,n-k_{1}.
Adding the probabilities we have

\begin{split}P_{q,S}^{(1)}(n)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{1}}P_{q,\theta}\Big(L_{n-k_{1}}^{(1)}&<k_{1}\ \wedge\ L_{n-k_{1}}^{(0)}<k_{2}\ \wedge\ F_{n-k_{1}}=i\ \wedge\ X_{n-k_{1}}=0\ \wedge\\
&X_{n-k_{1}+1}=\cdots=X_{n}=1\Big).\\
\end{split}

If the number of
0
’s in the first
n-k_{1}
trials is equal to
i,
that is,
F_{n-k_{1}}=i,
then in each of the
(n-k_{1}+1)
to
n
-th trials the probability of success is

\begin{split}p_{n-k_{1}+1}=\cdots=p_{n}=\theta q^{i}.\end{split}

We will write
E_{n,i}^{(0)}
for the event
\left\{L_{n}^{(1)}<k_{1}\ \wedge\ L_{n}^{(0)}<k_{2}\ \wedge\ X_{n}=0\ \wedge\ F_{n}=i\right\}.

We can now rewrite as follows.

\begin{split}P_{q,S}^{(1)}(n)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{1}}&P_{q,\theta}\Big(L_{n-k_{1}}^{(1)}<k_{1}\ \wedge\ L_{n-k_{1}}^{(0)}<k_{2}\ \wedge\ F_{n-k_{1}}=i\ \wedge\ X_{n-k_{1}}=0\Big)\\
&\times P_{q,\theta}\Big(X_{n-k_{1}+1}=\cdots=X_{n}=1\mid F_{n-k_{1}}=i\Big)\\
=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{1}}&P_{q,\theta}\Big(E_{n-k_{1},i}^{(0)}\Big)\Big(\theta q^{i}\Big)^{k_{1}}.\\
\end{split}

We are going to focus on the event
E_{n-k_{1},i}^{(0)}
. For
i=1,\ldots,n-k_{1}
, a typical element of the event
\{L_{n-k_{1}}^{(1)}<k_{1}\ \wedge\ L_{n-k_{1}}^{(0)}<k_{2}\ \wedge\ F_{n-k_{1}}=i\}
is an ordered sequence which consists of
n-k_{1}-i
successes and
i
failures such that the length of the longest success run is less than
k_{1}
and the length of the longest failure run is less than
k_{2}
. The number of these sequences can be derived as follows.
First we will distribute the
i
failures. Let
s

(1\leq s\leq i)
be the number of runs of failures in the typical element of the event
E_{n-k_{1},i}^{(0)}
. Next, we will distribute the
n-k_{1}-i
successes. We divide into two cases: starting with a failure run or starting with a success run. Thus, we distinguish between two types of sequences in the event

\left\{L_{n-k_{1}}^{(1)}<k_{1}\wedge\ L_{n-k_{1}}^{(0)}<k_{2}\wedge\ F_{n-k_{1}}=i\right\},

respectively named
(s-1,\ s)
-type and
(s,\ s)
-type, which are defined as follows.

\begin{split}(s-1,\ s)\text{-type}\ :&\quad\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{1}}\mid\overbrace{1\ldots 1}^{x_{1}}\mid\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{2}}\mid\overbrace{1\ldots 1}^{x_{2}}\mid\ldots\mid\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{s-1}}\mid\overbrace{1\ldots 1}^{x_{s-1}}\mid\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{s}},\\
\end{split}

with
i

0
’s and
n-k_{1}-i

1
’s, where
x_{j}

(j=1,\ldots,s-1)
represents a length of run of
1
’s and
y_{j}

(j=1,\ldots,s)
represents the length of a run of
0
’s. And all integers
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}
, and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
satisfy the conditions

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\mbox{\ for\ }j=1,...,s-1,\mbox{\ and\ }x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=n-k_{1}-i,\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\mbox{\ for\ }j=1,...,s,\mbox{\ and\ }y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=i.\end{split}

\begin{split}(s,\ s)\text{-type}\ :&\quad\overbrace{1\ldots 1}^{x_{1}}\mid\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{1}}\mid\overbrace{1\ldots 1}^{x_{2}}\mid\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{2}}\mid\overbrace{1\ldots 1}^{x_{3}}\mid\ldots\mid\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{s-1}}\mid\overbrace{1\ldots 1}^{x_{s}}\mid\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{s}},\end{split}

with
i

0
’s and
n-k_{1}-i

1
’s, where
x_{j}

(j=1,\ldots,s)
represents a length of run of
1
’s and
y_{j}

(j=1,\ldots,s)
represents the length of a run of
0
’s. Here all of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
are integers, and they satisfy

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\mbox{\ for\ }j=1,...,s,\mbox{\ and\ }x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=n-k_{1}-i,\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\mbox{\ for\ }j=1,...,s,\mbox{\ and\ }y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=i.\end{split}

Then the probability of the event
E_{n-k_{1},i}^{(0)}
is given by

\begin{split}P_{q,\theta}\Big(E_{n-k_{1},i}^{(0)}&\Big)=\\
\sum_{s=1}^{i}\Bigg[\bigg\{&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=n-k_{1}-i\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=i\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}\big(1-\theta q^{0}\big)\cdots\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}-1}\big)\times\\
&\Big(\theta q^{y_{1}}\Big)^{x_{1}}\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}}\big)\cdots\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}-1}\big)\times\\
&\Big(\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}}\Big)^{x_{2}}\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}}\big)\cdots\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}-1}\big)\times\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\vdots\\
&\Big(\theta q^{y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}}\Big)^{x_{s-1}}\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}}\big)\cdots\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}-1}\big)\bigg\}+\\
\end{split}

\begin{split}\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad&\bigg\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=n-k_{1}-i\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=i\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}\big(\theta q^{0}\big)^{x_{1}}\big(1-\theta q^{0}\big)\cdots(1-\theta q^{y_{1}-1})\times\\
&\Big(\theta q^{y_{1}}\Big)^{x_{2}}\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}}\big)\cdots\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}-1}\big)\times\\
&\Big(\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}}\Big)^{x_{3}}\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}}\big)\cdots\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}-1}\big)\times\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\vdots\\
&\Big(\theta q^{y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}}\Big)^{x_{s}}\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}}\big)\cdots\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}-1}\big)\bigg\}\Bigg].\\
\end{split}

Using simple exponentiation algebra arguments to simplify,

\begin{split}&P_{q,\theta}\Big(E_{n-k_{1},i}^{(0)}\Big)=\\
&\theta^{n-k_{1}-i}{\prod_{j=1}^{i}}\ \left(1-\theta q^{j-1}\right)\times\\
&\sum_{s=1}^{i}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=n-k_{1}-i\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=i\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s-1}}+\\
&\quad\quad\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=n-k_{1}-i\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=i\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s}}\Bigg].\\
\end{split}

Using Lemma
3.1
and Lemma
3.2
, we can rewrite as follows.

\begin{split}P_{q,\theta}\Big(E_{n-k_{1},i}^{(0)}\Big)=\theta^{n-k_{1}-i}{\prod_{j=1}^{i}}\left(1-\theta q^{j-1}\right)\sum_{s=1}^{i}\Bigg[A&{}_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{1}-i,\ i,\ s)+B_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{1}-i,\ i,\ s)\Bigg],\\
\end{split}

where

\begin{split}A_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n&-k_{1}-i,\ i,\ s)\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=n-k_{1}-i\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=i\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s-1}},\end{split}

and

\begin{split}B_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n&-k_{1}-i,\ i,\ s)\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=n-k_{1}-i\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=i\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s}}.\end{split}

Therefore we can compute the probability of the event
W_{S}^{(1)}=n
as follows.

\begin{split}P_{q,S}^{(1)}(n)=&\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{1}}P\Big(E_{n-k_{1},i}^{(0)}\Big)\Big(\theta q^{i}\Big)^{k_{1}}\\
=&\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{1}}\theta^{n-k_{1}-i}{\prod_{j=1}^{i}}\left(1-\theta q^{j-1}\right)\sum_{s=1}^{i}\bigg[A_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{1}-i,\ i,\ s)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+B_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{1}-i,\ i,\ s)\bigg]\Big(\theta q^{i}\Big)^{k_{1}}.\\
\end{split}

Finally, applying typical factorization algebra arguments,
P_{S}^{(1)}(n)
can be rewritten as

\begin{split}P_{q,S}^{(1)}(n)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{1}}\theta^{n-i}q^{ik_{1}}{\prod_{j=1}^{i}}(1-\theta q^{j-1})\sum_{s=1}^{i}\bigg[A_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{1}-i,\ i,\ s)+B_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{1}-i,\ i,\ s)\bigg].\\
\end{split}

Next, we are now going to study
P_{q,S}^{(0)}(n)
. It is easy to see that
P_{q,S}^{(0)}(k_{2})={\prod_{j=1}^{k_{2}}}(1-\theta q^{j-1})
. From now on we assume
n>k_{2},
and we can write
P_{q,S}^{(0)}(n)
as

\begin{split}P_{q,S}^{(0)}(n)&=P\left(L_{n-k_{2}}^{(1)}<k_{1}\ \wedge\ L_{n-k_{2}}^{(0)}<k_{2}\ \wedge\ X_{n-k_{2}}=1\ \wedge\ X_{n-k_{2}+1}=\cdots=X_{n}=0\right).\\
\end{split}

We partition the event
W_{S}^{(0)}=n
into disjoint events given by
F_{n-k_{2}}=i,
for
i=1,\ldots,n-k_{2}.
Adding the probabilities we have

\begin{split}P_{q,S}^{(0)}(n)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{2}}P_{q,\theta}\Big(L_{n-k_{2}}^{(1)}&<k_{1}\ \wedge\ L_{n-k_{2}}^{(0)}<k_{2}\ \wedge\ F_{n-k_{2}}=i\ \wedge\ X_{n-k_{2}}=1\ \wedge\\
&X_{n-k_{2}+1}=\cdots=X_{n}=0\Big).\\
\end{split}

If the number of
0
’s in the first
n-k_{2}
trials is equal to
i,
that is,
F_{n-k_{2}}=i,
then the probability of failures in all of the
(n-k_{2}+1)
-th to
n
-th trials is

\begin{split}P_{q,\theta}(X_{n-k_{2}+1}=\cdots=X_{n}=0\,\mid\,F_{n-k_{2}}=i)={\prod_{j=i+1}^{i+k_{2}}}(1-\theta q^{j-1}).\end{split}

We write
E_{n,i}^{(1)}
for the event
\left\{L_{n}^{(1)}<k_{1}\ \wedge\ L_{n}^{(0)}<k_{2}\ \wedge\ X_{n}=1\ \wedge\ F_{n}=i\right\}.
We can now rewrite as follows

\begin{split}P_{q,S}^{(0)}(n)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{2}}&P_{q,\theta}\Big(L_{n-k_{2}}^{(1)}<k_{1}\ \wedge\ L_{n-k_{2}}^{(0)}<k_{2}\ \wedge\ F_{n-k_{2}}=i\ \wedge\ X_{n-k_{2}}=1\Big)\\
&\times P_{q,\theta}\Big(X_{n-k_{2}+1}=\cdots=X_{n}=0\mid F_{n-k_{2}}=i\Big)\\
=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{2}}&P_{q,\theta}\Big(E_{n-k_{2},i}^{(1)}\Big){\prod_{j=i+1}^{i+k_{2}}}(1-\theta q^{j-1}).\\
\end{split}

We are going to focus on the event
E_{n-k_{2},i}^{(1)}
. For
i=1,\ldots,n-k_{2}
, a typical element of the event
\{L_{n-k_{1}}^{(1)}<k_{1}\wedge\ L_{n-k_{1}}^{(0)}<k_{2}\wedge\ F_{n-k_{1}}=i\}
is an ordered sequence which consists of
n-k_{2}-i
successes and
i
failures such that the length of the longest success run is less than
k_{1}
and the length of the longest failure run is less than
k_{2}
. The number of these sequences can be derived as follows.
First we will distribute the
i
failures. Let
s

(1\leq s\leq i)
be the number of runs of failures in the typical element of the event
E_{n-k_{2},i}^{(1)}
. Next, we will distribute the
n-k_{2}-i
successes. We divide into two cases: starting with a success run or starting with a failure run. Thus, we distinguish between two types of sequences in the event

\left\{L_{n-k_{1}}^{(1)}<k_{1}\wedge\ L_{n-k_{1}}^{(0)}<k_{2}\wedge\ F_{n-k_{1}}=i\right\},

respectively named
(s-1,\ s)
-type and
(s,\ s)
-type, which are defined as follows.

\begin{split}(s,\ s-1)\text{-type}\ :&\quad\overbrace{1\ldots 1}^{x_{1}}\mid\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{1}}\mid\overbrace{1\ldots 1}^{x_{2}}\mid\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{2}}\mid\ldots\mid\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{s-1}}\mid\overbrace{1\ldots 1}^{x_{s}},\\
\end{split}

with
i

0
’s and
n-k_{2}-i

1
’s, where
x_{j}

(j=1,\ldots,s)
represents the length of a run of
1
’s and
y_{j}

(j=1,\ldots,s-1)
represents the length of a run of
0
’s. And all integers
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}
, and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}
satisfy the conditions

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\mbox{\ for\ }j=1,...,s,\mbox{\ and\ }x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=n-k_{2}-i,\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\mbox{\ for\ }j=1,...,s-1,\mbox{\ and\ }y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}=i.\end{split}

\begin{split}(s,\ s)\text{-type}\ :&\quad\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{1}}\mid\overbrace{1\ldots 1}^{x_{1}}\mid\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{2}}\mid\overbrace{1\ldots 1}^{x_{2}}\mid\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{3}}\mid\ldots\mid\overbrace{0\ldots 0}^{y_{s}}\mid\overbrace{1\ldots 1}^{x_{s}},\end{split}

with
i

0
’s and
n-k_{2}-i

1
’s, where
x_{j}

(j=1,\ldots,s)
represents the length of a run of
1
’s and
y_{j}

(j=1,\ldots,s)
represents the length of a run of
0
’s. Here all of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
are integers, and they satisfy

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\mbox{\ for\ }j=1,...,s,\mbox{\ and\ }x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=n-k_{2}-i,\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\mbox{\ for\ }j=1,...,s,\mbox{\ and\ }y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=i.\end{split}

Then the probability of the event
E_{n-k_{2},i}^{(1)}
is given by

\begin{split}P_{q,\theta}\Big(E_{n-k_{2},i}^{(1)}&\Big)=\\
\sum_{s=1}^{n-k_{2}-i}\Bigg[\bigg\{&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=n-k_{2}-i\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}=i\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}\big(\theta q^{0}\big)^{x_{1}}\big(1-\theta q^{0}\big)\cdots(1-\theta q^{y_{1}-1})\times\\
&\Big(\theta q^{y_{1}}\Big)^{x_{2}}\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}}\big)\cdots\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}-1}\big)\times\\
&\Big(\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}}\Big)^{x_{3}}\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}}\big)\cdots\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}-1}\big)\times\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\vdots\\
&\Big(\theta q^{y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}}\Big)^{x_{s}}\bigg\}+\\
\end{split}

\begin{split}\quad\quad\quad\quad&\bigg\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=n-k_{2}-i\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=i\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}\big(1-\theta q^{0}\big)\cdots\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}-1}\big)\Big(\theta q^{y_{1}}\Big)^{x_{1}}\times\\
&\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}}\big)\cdots\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}-1}\big)\Big(\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}}\Big)^{x_{2}}\times\\
&\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}}\big)\cdots\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}-1}\big)\Big(\theta q^{y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}}\Big)^{x_{3}}\times\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\vdots\\
&\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}}\big)\cdots\big(1-\theta q^{y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}-1}\big)\Big(\theta q^{y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}}\Big)^{x_{s}}\bigg\}\Bigg].\\
\end{split}

Using simple exponentiation algebra arguments to simplify,

\begin{split}&P_{q,\theta}\Big(E_{n-k_{2},i}^{(1)}\Big)=\\
&\theta^{n-k_{2}-i}{\prod_{j=1}^{i}}\ (1-\theta q^{j-1})\times\\
&\sum_{s=1}^{n-k_{2}-i}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=n-k_{2}-i\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}=i\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s}}+\\
&\quad\quad\quad\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=n-k_{2}-i\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=i\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s})x_{s}}\Bigg].\\
\end{split}

Using Lemma
3.3
and Lemma
3.4
, we can rewrite as follows.

\begin{split}P_{q,\theta}\Big(E_{n-k_{2},i}^{(1)}\Big)=\theta^{n-k_{2}-i}{\prod_{j=1}^{i}}(1-\theta q^{j-1})\sum_{s=1}^{n-k_{2}-i}\Bigg[C&{}_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{2}-i,\ i,\ s)+D_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{2}-i,\ i,\ s)\Bigg],\\
\end{split}

where

\begin{split}C_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n&-k_{2}-i,\ i,\ s)\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=n-k_{2}-i\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1}=i\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s}},\end{split}

and

\begin{split}D_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n&-k_{2}-i,\ i,\ s)\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s}=n-k_{2}-i\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s}=i\\
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\end{subarray}}q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s})x_{s}}.\end{split}

Therefore we can compute the probability of the event
W_{S}^{(0)}=n
as follows

\begin{split}P_{q,S}^{(0)}(n)=&\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{2}}P_{q,\theta}\Big(E_{n-k_{2},i}^{(1)}\Big){\prod_{j=i+1}^{i+k_{2}}}(1-\theta q^{j-1})\\
=&\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{2}}\theta^{n-k_{2}-i}{\prod_{j=1}^{i}}\left(1-\theta q^{j-1}\right)\sum_{s=1}^{n-k_{2}-i}\bigg[C_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{2}-i,\ i,\ s)+D_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{2}-i,\ i,\ s)\bigg]\\
&\times{\prod_{j=i+1}^{i+k_{2}}}\left(1-\theta q^{j-1}\right).\\
\end{split}

Finally, applying typical factorization algebra arguments,
P_{q,S}^{(0)}(n)
can be rewritten as follows

\begin{split}P_{q,S}^{(0)}(n)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{2}}\theta^{n-k_{2}-i}{\prod_{j=1}^{i+k_{2}}}(1-\theta q^{j-1})\sum_{s=1}^{n-k_{2}-i}\bigg[C_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{2}-i,\ i,\ s)+D_{q}^{k_{1},k_{2}}(n-k_{2}-i,\ i,\ s)\bigg].\\
\end{split}

Thus proof is completed.
∎

It is worth mentioning here that the PMF
f_{q,S}(n;\theta)
approaches the probability function of the sooner waiting time distribution of order
(k_{1},k_{2})

in the limit as
q
tends to 1 when a run(succession) run quota is imposed on runs of successes and failures of IID model. The details are presented in the following remark.

Remark 5
.

For
q=1
, the PMF
f_{q,S}(n;\theta)
reduces to the PMF
f_{S}(n;\theta)=P_{\theta}(W_{S}=n)
for
n\geq min(k_{1},k_{2})
is given by

\begin{split}P_{\theta}(W_{S}=n)=P_{S}^{(1)}(n)+P_{S}^{(0)}(n),\end{split}

where
P_{S}^{(1)}(k_{1})=\theta^{k_{1}}
,
P_{S}^{(0)}(k_{2})=(1-\theta)^{k_{2}}
,

\begin{split}P_{S}^{(1)}(n)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{1}}\theta^{n-i}(1-\theta)^{i}\sum_{s=1}^{i}S(s,k_{2},i)\bigg[&S(s-1,k_{1},n-k_{1}-i)\\
&+S(s-1,k_{1},n-k_{1}-i)\bigg],\ n>k_{1}\\
\end{split}

and

\begin{split}P_{S}^{(0)}(n)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k_{2}}\theta^{n-k_{2}-i}(1-\theta)^{i+k_{2}}\sum_{s=1}^{n-k_{2}-i}S(s,k_{1},n-k_{2}-i)\bigg[S(s-1,k_{2},i)+S(s,k_{2},i)\bigg],\ n>k_{2}.\\
\end{split}

where

\begin{split}S(a,\ b,\ c)=\sum_{j=0}^{min(a,\left[\frac{c-a}{b-1}\right])}(-1)^{j}{a\choose j}{c-j(b-1)-1\choose a-1}.\end{split}

See, e.g.
Charalambides, 2002
.

3.2
q
-later waiting time distribution of order
(k_{1},k_{2})

The problem of waiting time that will be discussed in this section is one of the ’later cases’ and it emerges when a quota is imposed on runs of successes and failures. More specifically, Binary (zero and one) trials with probability of ones varying according to a geometric rule, are performed sequentially until
k_{1}
consecutive successes and
k_{2}
consecutive failures are observed, whichever event gets observed later. Let
W_{L}^{(1)}
be a random variable denoting that the waiting time until
k_{1}
consecutive successes are observed for the first time and failure run of length
k_{2}
or more has appeared before and
W_{L}^{(0)}
be a random variable denoting that the waiting time until
k_{2}
consecutive failures are observed for the first time and success run of length
k_{1}
or more has appeared before.

We will also simply write
f^{(1)}_{q,L}(n;\theta)=P_{q,\theta}(W_{L}^{(1)}=n)
and
f^{(0)}_{q,L}(n;\theta)=P_{q,\theta}(W_{L}^{(0)}=n)
. Therefore we can write the probability of event
W_{L}=n
as follows

\begin{split}P_{q,\theta}(W_{L}=n)=f^{(1)}_{q,L}(n;\theta)+f^{(0)}_{q,L}(n;\theta),\ n\geq k_{1}+k_{2}.\end{split}

We now make some useful Definition and Lemma for the proofs of Theorem in the sequel.

Definition 3.5
.

For
s,r,k\in\mathbb{N}
we define

\begin{split}S_{r,s}^{k}=\left\{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{s})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{s}\ \mid\ \sum_{j=1}^{s}i_{j}=r\ \wedge\ \max(i_{1},\ldots,i_{s})>k\right\}.\end{split}

Definition 3.6
.

For
0<q\leq 1
, we define

\begin{split}\overline{E}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)={\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}}}\ \sum_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}}q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s-1}},\ \end{split}

where the summation is over all integers
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1},
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
satisfying

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s-1,\end{split}

\begin{split}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})\in S_{u,s-1}^{0},\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\in S_{v,s}^{0}.\end{split}

The following gives a recurrence relation useful for the computation of
\overline{E}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)
.

Lemma 3.5
.

\overline{E}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)
obeys the following recurrence relation.

\begin{split}\overline{E}&{}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)\\
&=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{b=1}^{v-(s-1)}\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{a(v-b)}\overline{E}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u-a,v-b,s-1),&\text{for}\ s>1,\ s-1\leq u\leq(s-1)(k_{1}-1)\\
&\text{and}\ s\leq v\\
1,&\text{for}\ s=1,\ u=0,\ \text{and}\ 1\leq v\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{split}

Proof.

For
s>1
,
s-1\leq u\leq(s-1)(k_{1}-1)
and
s\leq v
, we observe that
x_{s-1}
may assume any value
1,\ldots,k_{1}-1
, then
\overline{E}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)
can be written as

\begin{split}\overline{E}&{}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)\\
=&\sum_{x_{s-1}=1}^{k_{1}-1}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-2}<k_{1}\\
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-2})\in S_{u-x_{s-1},s-2}^{0}\end{subarray}}}{\hskip 2.84544pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\in S_{v,s}^{0}\end{subarray}}}q^{x_{s-1}(v-y_{s})}\ q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-2}}\end{split}

Similarly, we observe that since
y_{s}
can assume the values
1,\ldots,v-(s-1)
, then
\overline{E}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)
can be rewritten as

\begin{split}\overline{E}&{}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)\\
=&\sum_{y_{s}=1}^{v-(s-1)}\sum_{x_{s-1}=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{x_{s-1}(v-y_{s})}{\hskip-28.45274pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-2}<k_{1}\\
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-2})\in S_{u-x_{s-1},s-2}^{0}\end{subarray}}}{\hskip 2.84544pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})\in S_{v-y_{s},s-1}^{0}\end{subarray}}}q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-2}}\\
=&\sum_{b=1}^{v-(s-1)}\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{a(v-b)}\overline{E}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u-a,v-b,s-1).\end{split}

The other cases are obvious and thus the proof is completed.
∎

Remark 6
.

We observe that for
\overline{E}_{1,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)
is the number of integer solutions
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})
and
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})
of

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s-1,\end{split}

\begin{split}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})\in S_{u,s-1}^{0},\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\in S_{r,s}^{0}\end{split}

which is

\begin{split}\overline{E}_{1,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)=S(s-1,\ k_{1},\ u)M(s,\ v),\end{split}

where
S(a,\ b,\ c)
denotes the total number of integer solution
x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{a}=c
such that
0<x_{i}<b
for
i=1,2,\ldots,a
. The number is given by

\begin{split}S(a,\ b,\ c)=\sum_{j=0}^{min\left(a,\ \left[\frac{c-a}{b-1}\right]\right)}(-1)^{j}{a\choose j}{c-j(b-1)-1\choose a-1}.\end{split}

where
M(a,\ b)
denotes the total number of integer solution
y_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+y_{a}=b
such that
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{a})\in S_{b,a}^{0}
. The number is given by

\begin{split}M(a,\ b)={b-1\choose a-1}.\end{split}

See, e.g.
Charalambides, 2002
.

Definition 3.7
.

For
0<q\leq 1
, we define

\begin{split}E_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)={\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}}}\ \sum_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}}q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s-1}},\ \end{split}

where the summation is over all integers
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1},
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
satisfying

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s-1,\end{split}

\begin{split}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})\in S_{m,s-1}^{0},\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\in S_{r,s}^{k_{2}-1}.\end{split}

The following gives a recurrence relation useful for the computation of
E_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)
.

Lemma 3.6
.

E_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)
obeys the following recurrence relation.

\begin{split}E&{}_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)\\
&=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{a(r-b)}E_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m-a,r-b,s-1)\\
+\sum_{b=k_{2}}^{r-(s-1)}\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{a(r-b)}\overline{E}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(m-a,r-b,s-1),&\text{for}\ s>1,\ s-1\leq m\leq(s-1)(k_{1}-1)\\
&\text{and}\ s-1+k_{2}\leq r\\
1,&\text{for}\ s=1,\ m=0,\ \text{and}\ k_{2}\leq r\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{split}

Proof.

For
s>1
,
s-1\leq m\leq(s-1)(k_{1}-1)
and
s-1+k_{2}\leq r
, we observe that
x_{s-1}
may assume any value
1,\ldots,k_{1}-1
, then
E_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)
can be written as

\begin{split}E&{}_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)\\
=&\sum_{x_{s-1}=1}^{k_{1}-1}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-2}<k_{1}\\
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-2})\in S_{m-x_{s-1},s-2}^{0}\end{subarray}}}\ {\hskip 2.84544pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\in S_{r,s}^{k_{2}-1}\end{subarray}}}q^{x_{s-1}(r-y_{s})}\ q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-2}}\end{split}

Similarly, we observe that since
y_{s}
can assume the values
1,\ldots,k_{2}-1
, then
E_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)
can be rewritten as

\begin{split}E&{}_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)\\
=&\sum_{y_{s}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{x_{s-1}=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{x_{s-1}(r-y_{s})}{\hskip-2.84544pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-2}<k_{1}\\
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-2})\in S_{m-x_{s-1},s-2}^{0}\end{subarray}}}\ {\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})\in S_{r-y_{s},s-1}^{k_{2}-1}\end{subarray}}}\ q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-2}}\\
&+\sum_{y_{s}=k_{2}}^{r-(s-1)}\sum_{x_{s-1}=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{x_{s-1}(r-y_{s})}{\hskip-28.45274pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-2}<k_{1}\\
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-2})\in S_{m-x_{s-1},s-2}^{0}\end{subarray}}}\ {\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})\in S_{r-y_{s},s-1}^{0}\end{subarray}}}\ q^{y_{1}x_{1}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{2}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-2}}\\
=&\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{a(r-b)}E_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m-a,r-b,s-1)+\sum_{b=k_{2}}^{r-(s-1)}\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{a(r-b)}\overline{E}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(m-a,r-b,s-1).\end{split}

The other cases are obvious and thus the proof is completed.
∎

Remark 7
.

We observe that for
E_{1,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)
is the number of integer solutions
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})
and
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})
of

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s-1,\end{split}

\begin{split}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})\in S_{m,s-1}^{0},\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\in S_{r,s}^{k_{2}-1}\end{split}

which is

\begin{split}E_{1,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)=S(s-1,\ k_{1},\ m)R(s,\ k_{2},\ r),\end{split}

where
S(a,\ b,\ c)
denotes the total number of integer solution
x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{a}=c
such that
0<x_{i}<b
for
i=1,2,\ldots,a
. The number is given by

\begin{split}S(a,\ b,\ c)=\sum_{j=0}^{min\left(a,\ \left[\frac{c-a}{b-1}\right]\right)}(-1)^{j}{a\choose j}{c-j(b-1)-1\choose a-1}.\end{split}

where
R(a,\ b,\ c)
denotes the total number of integer solution
y_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+y_{a}=c
such that
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{a})\in S_{c,a}^{b-1}
. Alternatively, it is the number of ways in which
c
identical objects can be arranged to form
a
groups with at least one group containing more than or equal to
b
objects. The number is given by

\begin{split}R(a,\ b,\ c)=\sum_{j=1}^{min\left(a,\ \left[\frac{c-a}{b-1}\right]\right)}(-1)^{j+1}{a\choose j}{c-j(b-1)-1\choose a-1}.\end{split}

See, e.g.
Charalambides, 2002
.

Definition 3.8
.

For
0<q\leq 1
, we define

\begin{split}\overline{F}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)={\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}}}\ \sum_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}}q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s}},\ \end{split}

where the summation is over all integers
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
satisfying

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s,\end{split}

\begin{split}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})\in S_{m,s}^{0},\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\in S_{r,s}^{0}.\end{split}

The following gives a recurrence relation useful for the computation of
\overline{F}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)
.

Lemma 3.7
.

\overline{F}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)
obeys the following recurrence relation.

\begin{split}\overline{F}&{}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)\\
&=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{b=1}^{v-(s-1)}\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{a(v-b)}\overline{F}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u-a,v-b,s-1),&\text{for}\ s>1,\ s\leq u\leq s(k_{1}-1)\\
&\text{and}\ s\leq v\\
1,&\text{for}\ s=1,\ 1\leq u\leq k_{1}-1,\ \text{and}\ 1\leq v\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{split}

Proof.

For
s>1
,
s\leq u\leq s(k_{1}-1)
and
s\leq v
, we observe that
x_{s}
may assume any value
1,\ldots,k_{1}-1
, then
\overline{F}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)
can be written as

\begin{split}\overline{F}&{}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)\\
=&\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{k_{1}-1}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}<k_{1}\\
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})\in S_{u-x_{s},s-1}^{0}\end{subarray}}}\ {\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\in S_{v,s}^{0}\end{subarray}}}q^{x_{s}(v-y_{s})}\ q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}\end{split}

Similarly, we observe that since
y_{s}
can assume the values
1,\ldots,v-(s-1)
, then
\overline{F}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)
can be rewritten as

\begin{split}\overline{F}&{}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u,v,s)\\
=&\sum_{y_{s}=1}^{v-(s-1)}\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{x_{s}(v-y_{s})}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-2}<k_{1}\\
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-2})\in S_{u-x_{s-1},s-2}^{0}\end{subarray}}}\ {\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})\in S_{v-y_{s},s-1}^{0}\end{subarray}}}q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}\\
=&\sum_{b=1}^{v-(s-1)}\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{a(v-b)}\overline{F}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(u-a,v-b,s-1).\end{split}

The other cases are obvious and thus the proof is completed.
∎

\Box

Remark 8
.

We observe that
\overline{F}_{1,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)
is the number of integer solutions
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
and
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})
of

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s,\end{split}

\begin{split}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})\in S_{u,s}^{0},\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\in S_{r,s}^{0}\end{split}

which is

\begin{split}\overline{F}_{1,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)=S(s,\ k_{1},\ u)M(s,\ v),\end{split}

where
S(a,\ b,\ c)
denotes the total number of integer solution
x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{a}=c
such that
0<x_{i}<b
for
i=1,2,\ldots,a
. The number is given by

\begin{split}S(a,\ b,\ c)=\sum_{j=0}^{min\left(a,\ \left[\frac{c-a}{b-1}\right]\right)}(-1)^{j}{a\choose j}{c-j(b-1)-1\choose a-1}.\end{split}

where
M(a,\ b)
denotes the total number of integer solution

such that

. The number is given by

See, e.g.
Charalambides, 2002
.

Definition 3.9
.

For
0<q\leq 1
, we define

\begin{split}F_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)={\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}}}\ \sum_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}}q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s}},\ \end{split}

where the summation is over all integers
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
satisfying

\begin{split}0<x_{j}<k_{1},\ \text{for}j=1,\ \ldots,\ s,\end{split}

\begin{split}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})\in S_{m,s}^{0},\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\in S_{r,s}^{k_{2}-1}.\end{split}

The following gives a recurrence relation useful for the computation of
F_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)
.

Lemma 3.8
.

F_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)
obeys the following recurrence relation.

\begin{split}F&{}_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)\\
&=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}\ \sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{a(r-b)}F_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m-a,r-b,s-1)\\
+\sum_{b=k_{2}}^{r-(s-1)}\ \sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{a(r-b)}\overline{F}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(m-a,r-b,s-1),&\text{for}\ s>1,\ s\leq m\leq s(k_{1}-1)\\
&\text{and}\ (s-1)+k_{2}\leq r\\
1,&\text{for}\ s=1,\ 0<m<k_{1},\ \text{and}\ k_{2}\leq r\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{split}

Proof.

For
s>1
,
s\leq m\leq s(k_{1}-1)
and
(s-1)+k_{2}\leq r
, we observe that
x_{s}
may assume any value
1,\ldots,k_{1}-1
, then
F_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)
can be written as

\begin{split}F&{}_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)\\
=&\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{k_{1}-1}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=m-x_{s}\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}}\quad{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\in S_{r,s}^{k_{2}-1}\end{subarray}}}q^{x_{s}(r-y_{s})}\ q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}\end{split}

Similarly, we observe that since
y_{s}
can assume the values
1,\ldots,r-(s-1)
, then
F_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)
can be rewritten as

\begin{split}F&{}_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m,r,s)\\
=&\sum_{y_{s}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{x_{s}(r-y_{s})}{\hskip-8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=m-x_{s}\\
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}}\quad{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})\in S_{r-y_{s},s-1}^{k_{2}-1}\end{subarray}}}q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}\\
&+\sum_{y_{s}=k_{2}}^{r-(s-1)}\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{x_{s}(r-y_{s})}{\hskip-8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{s-1}=m-x_{s}\\
x_{1},\ \ldots,\ x_{s-1}\in\{1,\ \ldots,\ k_{1}-1\}\end{subarray}}}\quad{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})\in S_{r-y_{s},s-1}^{0}\end{subarray}}}\hskip-14.22636ptq^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}\\
=&\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}\ \sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{a(r-b)}F_{q,0,k_{2}}^{k_{1},\infty}(m-a,r-b,s-1)+\sum_{b=k_{2}}^{r-(s-1)}\ \sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{a(r-b)}\overline{F}_{q,0,0}^{k_{1},\infty}(m-a,r-b,s-1).\end{split}

The other cases are obvious and thus the proof is completed.
∎

\Box

Definition 3.10
.

For
0<q\leq 1
, we define

\begin{split}\overline{G}_{q,0,0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(u,v,s)={\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}}}\ \sum_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}}q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s}},\ \end{split}

where the summation is over all integers
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}
satisfying

\begin{split}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})\in S_{u,s}^{0},\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s-1,\end{split}

\begin{split}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})\in S_{v,s-1}^{0}.\end{split}

The following gives a recurrence relation useful for the computation of
\overline{G}_{q,0,0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(u,v,s)
.

Lemma 3.9
.

\overline{G}_{q,0,0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(u,v,s)
obeys the following recurrence relation.

\begin{split}\overline{G}&{}_{q,0,0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(u,v,s)\\
&=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{a=1}^{u-(s-1)}\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}q^{va}\overline{G}_{q,0,0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(u-a,v-b,s-1),&\text{for}\ s>1,\ s\leq u\\
&\text{and}\ s-1\leq v\leq(s-1)(k_{2}-1)\\
1,&\text{for}\ s=1,\ 1\leq u,\ \text{and}\ v=0\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{split}

Proof.

For
s>1
,
s\leq u
and
s-1\leq v\leq(s-1)(k_{2}-1)
, we observe that
x_{s}
may assume any value
1,\ldots,u-(s-1)
, then
\overline{G}_{q,0,0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(u,v,s)
can be written as

\begin{split}\overline{G}&{}_{q,0,0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(u,v,s)\\
=&\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{u-(s-1)}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})\in S_{u-x_{s},s-1}^{0}\end{subarray}}}\ {\hskip 2.84544pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\\
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})\in S_{v,s-1}^{0}\end{subarray}}}q^{vx_{s}}q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}.\end{split}

Similarly, we observe that since
y_{s}
can assume the values
1,\ldots,k_{2}-1
, then
\overline{G}_{q,0,0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(u,v,s)
can be rewritten as

\begin{split}\overline{G}&{}_{q,0,0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(u,v,s)\\
=&\sum_{y_{s-1}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{u-(s-1)}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})\in S_{u-x_{s},s-1}^{0}\end{subarray}}}\ {\hskip 2.84544pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-2}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\\
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-2})\in S_{v-y_{s-1},s-2}^{0}\end{subarray}}}q^{vx_{s}}\ q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}\\
=&\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{a=1}^{u-(s-1)}q^{va}\overline{G}_{q,0,0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(u-a,v-b,s-1)\end{split}

The other cases are obvious and thus the proof is completed.
∎

Remark 10
.

We observe that
\overline{G}_{1,0,0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(u,v,s)
is the number of integer solutions
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
and
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})
of

\begin{split}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})\in S_{u,s}^{0},\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s-1,\end{split}

\begin{split}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})\in S_{v,s-1}^{0}\end{split}

which is

\begin{split}\overline{G}_{1,0,0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(u,v,s)=M(s,\ u)S(s-1,\ k_{2},\ v),\end{split}

where
M(a,\ b)
denotes the total number of integer solution
x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{a}=b
such that
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{a})\in S_{b,a}^{0}
. The number is given by

\begin{split}M(a,\ b)={b-1\choose a-1}.\end{split}

where
S(a,\ b,\ c)
denotes the total number of integer solution
y_{1}+y_{2}+\cdots+y_{a}=c
such that
0<y_{i}<b
for
i=1,2,\ldots,a
. The number is given by

\begin{split}S(a,\ b,\ c)=\sum_{j=0}^{min\left(a,\ \left[\frac{c-a}{b-1}\right]\right)}(-1)^{j}{a\choose j}{c-j(b-1)-1\choose a-1}.\end{split}

See, e.g.
Charalambides, 2002
.

Definition 3.11
.

We define

\begin{split}G_{q,k_{1},0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(m,r,s)={\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}}}\ \sum_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}}q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-1})x_{s}},\ \end{split}

where the summation is over all integers
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}
satisfying

\begin{split}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})\in S_{m,s}^{k_{1}-1},\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s-1,\end{split}

\begin{split}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})\in S_{r,s-1}^{0}.\end{split}

The following gives a recurrence relation useful for the computation of
G_{q,k_{1},0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(m,r,s)
.

Lemma 3.10
.

G_{q,k_{1},0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(m,r,s)
obeys the following recurrence relation.

\begin{split}G&{}_{q,k_{1},0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
&=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{ra}G_{q,k_{1},0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(m-a,r-b,s-1)\\
+\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{a=k_{1}}^{m-(s-1)}q^{ra}\overline{G}_{q,0,0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(m-a,r-b,s-1),&\text{for}\ s>1,\ (s-1)+k_{1}\leq m\\
&\text{and}\ s-1\leq r\leq(s-1)(k_{2}-1)\\
1,&\text{for}\ s=1,\ k_{1}\leq m,\ \text{and}\ r=0\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{split}

Proof.

For
s>1
,
(s-1)+k_{1}\leq m
and
s-1\leq r\leq(s-1)(k_{2}-1)
, we observe that
x_{s}
may assume any value
1,\ldots,m-(s-1)
, then
G_{q,k_{1},0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(m,r,s)
can be written as

\begin{split}G&{}_{q,k_{1},0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
=&\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{k_{1}-1}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})\in S_{m-x_{s},s-1}^{k_{1}-1}\end{subarray}}}{\hskip 8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\\
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})\in S_{r,s-1}^{0}\end{subarray}}}q^{rx_{s}}\ \ q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}\\
&+\sum_{x_{s}=k_{1}}^{m-(s-1)}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})\in S_{m-x_{s},s-1}^{0}\end{subarray}}}{\hskip 8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\\
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})\in S_{r,s-1}^{0}\end{subarray}}}q^{rx_{s}}\ \ q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}.\end{split}

Similarly, we observe that since
y_{s-1}
can assume the values
1,\ldots,k_{2}-1
, then
G_{q,k_{1},0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(m,r,s)
can be rewritten as

\begin{split}G&{}_{q,k_{1},0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(m,r,s)\\
=&\sum_{y_{s-1}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{x_{s}=1}^{k_{1}-1}{\hskip 8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})\in S_{m-x_{s},s-1}^{k_{1}-1}\end{subarray}}}\ {\hskip 8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-2}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\\
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-2})\in S_{r-y_{s-1},s-2}^{0}\end{subarray}}}q^{rx_{s}}\ \ q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}\\
&+\sum_{y_{s-1}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{x_{s}=k_{1}}^{m-(s-1)}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})\in S_{m-x_{s},s-1}^{0}\end{subarray}}}{\hskip 8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-2}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}\\
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-2})\in S_{r-y_{s-1},s-2}^{0}\end{subarray}}}q^{rx_{s}}\ \ q^{y_{1}x_{2}+(y_{1}+y_{2})x_{3}+\cdots+(y_{1}+\cdots+y_{s-2})x_{s-1}}\\
=&\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}\sum_{a=1}^{k_{1}-1}q^{ra}G_{q,k_{1},0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(u-a,v-b,s-1)+\sum_{b=1}^{k_{2}-1}\ \sum_{a=k_{1}}^{m-(s-1)}q^{ra}\overline{G}_{q,0,0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(u-a,v-b,s-1).\end{split}

The other cases are obvious and thus the proof is completed.
∎

Remark 11
.

We observe that
G_{1,k_{1},0}^{\infty,k_{2}}(m,r,s)
is the number of integer solutions
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
and
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s-1})
of

\begin{split}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})\in S_{m,s}^{k_{1}-1},\ \text{and}\end{split}

\begin{split}0<y_{j}<k_{2}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\ldots,s-1,\end{split}

where

denotes the total number of integer solution
